| G14 | (row M12's left items) access control leaves out: the access of the base classes on the way to a member (11.2: a public member of a private base named through the derived class), protected and private destructors, operators other than `=` called as members, member types (a protected typedef or nested class, `B::T`, named anywhere: never checked), a friend function template defined in a class and a friend that names nothing declared (the class then goes unchecked), and every specialisation of a befriended function template name counts as a friend | fixed (2026-11-13, and the rest 2026-11-18). Measured first with one probe per item (cl /permissive- and clang -std=c++98 refuse the same lines but one): base classes 7 lines, destructors 4, operators and conversion functions 8 (cppparse reported 2, `()` and `+=`), member types 6, the friend template defined in a class 1; clang alone refuses `tf<long>` when `friend int tf<>(int);` names one specialisation, cl takes every specialisation for a friend, as cppparse does. 2026-11-13, operators: an operator function chosen for an expression is access-checked like any member, through the object's class (Sema::classOperator, where only `=` and the compound assignments were checked after it: `+`, `==`, `[]`, `++` in both forms, `!` and the other unary ones, `,`), and so are `operator->` (exprMember), a conversion function wherever one is applied (an implicit conversion in Sema::converted, which takes the scope now, an explicit cast, a condition in boolContext) and a class's own `operator new` in a new-expression, plain or placement (exprNew). access-refused.cpp lines 46 to 56 (eleven more S150, the lines cl and clang refuse) and front2.cpp `opaccess::` (private operators and a conversion function used by members and by a friend, protected ones by a derived class on its own object; against cl at -O0 and -O2); a scan of the 39 own sources, libcpp and the samples with the new check reports no S150. 2026-11-13, member types: a typedef, a nested class or enumeration and a member class template named by a type specifier are checked (Sema::namedType, memberTypeAccess) where the context of the name is known: in a body or an initialiser, in the member declarations of a class, and in a declaration at namespace scope, where the check is held back until the declarators have said what is declared (flushTypeAccess): 11/5 checks the names in a member's definition outside its class as in the class (`A::PT A::priv()`, `A::PT A::sp = 4;`), and a friend's own declaration may name them (`G::T fr(G::T x)`). Not checked: the signature of a function template and of its specialisations (an error there would be a failed substitution, and the specialisation that may be the friend does not exist yet), and the components of a qualifier (`A::Priv::Inner` checks `Inner` alone). 2026-11-13, destructors: an object destroyed where its class's destructor cannot be reached is refused (Sema::destructorAccess, through the object's own class, 11.5; an implicit destructor is public): an automatic or static local and a handler's object (localDecl), an object of namespace scope (declareSimple), the temporary of `T(args)` and of a cast to a class, and the operand of `delete` and `delete[]`. Not checked: the temporaries of calls that return a class and of arguments passed by value, the members and bases an implicit destructor destroys, a thrown object. sample-front/access-refused2.cpp (test 17: seventeen S150, nine for member types and eight for destructors, the lines cl and clang refuse) and front2.cpp `typeaccess::` and `dtoraccess::` (the class, a derived class, friends, out-of-class definitions, a derived class's implicit destructor over a protected one; against cl at -O0 and -O2); the scan of the own sources, libcpp and the samples reports no S150. The unit is a second one because the driver prints 30 diagnostics of a unit at most. 2026-11-13, the friend function template defined in a class: `template <class T> friend int peek(F& f, T t) { ... }` was not declared at all, so a call of it was S103 "call to undeclared function" (valid C++98 refused; cl and clang find it by argument-dependent lookup), and it left the class unchecked. It is now a function template of the innermost enclosing namespace (Sema::declareTemplate), hidden from ordinary lookup until that namespace declares it and found by argument-dependent lookup (11.4/5, 7.3.1.2/3), and a friend by its name as the declared ones are. Its body is in the lexical scope of the class: the instantiation's scope takes the class's members as a using-directive would (instantiateFunctionTemplate), so an unqualified `bonus()` finds the class's static member (an inherited one too), while the specialisations keep the namespace's names (`??$peek@H@friendtmpl@@YAHAEAUF@0@H@Z`, as cl's object has them). front2.cpp `friendtmpl::` (a template defined in the class and called with two argument types, one declared only and defined after the class, an `operator==` template defined in the class; against cl at -O0 and -O2) and access-refused2.cpp line 26 (the class is checked again: eighteen S150 in the unit). Not done: such a template in a class template, where every instantiation would define it again (the class stays unchecked, as before); a parenthesised name, `(peek)(f, 2)`, still finds it (cl and clang refuse it: no argument-dependent lookup). 2026-11-13, base classes (11.2): a member named in a class that reaches the member's class through a base that is not public has the access the base specifiers leave it, and Sema::accessible applies 11.2/4 to it: the naming class is the object's (`d.x`, `d.f()`), the qualifier's for a static member, a static member function or an enumerator (`D1::s`, `D1::sf()`), else the class of the member function the reference is in (the implicit this) or of the class being declared; accessIn gives the member's access as a member of that class (a public base keeps it, a protected one makes public protected, a private one makes both private; a member private in a base is no member one can name further down; the most accessible path counts, 11.7; a using-declaration in a class gives the member the access of the declaration, `Symbol::usingMembers`), and accessibleNamedIn the four rules (public; private or protected for the members and friends of the naming class; protected for the members and friends of a class derived from it in which the member is protected or private; or through a base class that is accessible at the point of the reference). A class with no protected or private base anywhere above it (`Symbol::nonPublicBases`) takes the old path, so the check costs a flag test there. A conversion from a derived class to a base, of a pointer, of a reference or of the object, needs the base to be accessible where it is made (11.2/4: an invented public member of the base would be): checked where the conversion that was chosen is applied (Sema::converted, baseConversionAccess: arguments, initialisers, assignments, returns), S150 "conversion from 'D1' to its base class 'B1': the base class is not accessible here (cl C2243)"; a public member out of reach is S150 "'B1::x': not accessible here: 'B1' is reached through a private or protected base class (cl C2247)". sample-front/access-refused3.cpp (test 17: nine S150, the lines cl and clang refuse: a data member, a member function, a static member and a static member function through a private base, a data member through a protected one and from a class derived from the private derivation, a pointer, a reference and a copy of the base part) and front2.cpp `baseaccess::` (the class's own members and a friend reach the private base and convert to it, a protected base stays reachable further down, two using-declarations, a public base; against cl at -O0 and -O2); the scan of the own sources, libcpp and the samples reports no S150. `p->B1::x` on a `D1*` is refused as well (the object's class is the naming class there, where cl and clang say the conversion to B1 is what fails). Not checked, both refused by cl and clang: a static_cast to an inaccessible base (a C-style cast may reach it, 5.4/7) and a member type named with a qualifier through such a base (`D1::T`). 2026-11-18, the rest, each item measured against cl /permissive- and clang -std=c++98 first and fixed where both refuse the code. A class's `operator delete` or `operator delete[]`, its own or inherited, is checked where a delete-expression names it and where a new-expression does (5.3.4/18: it frees the storage when the constructor throws), not for `::delete` and `::new` (Sema::deallocationAccess). `static_cast` of a pointer or a reference to an inaccessible base and down from it is S150 "static_cast between 'D2' and its base class 'B2': the base class is not accessible here" (Sema::baseClassAccess, which baseConversionAccess shares); a C-style cast stays (5.4/7). A member type named with a qualifier is named in the qualifier's class, so `D3::T` through a private or protected base is refused (memberTypeAccess takes the naming class), and each component of a qualifier that is a member type is checked in the class before it, in a type and in an expression (`A4::Priv::Inner`, `A4::Priv::value`: Sema::qualifierAccess over a trace that scopeForQualifier leaves). The temporary of a call that returns a class and the parameters a call copies class arguments to are destroyed by the caller: destructorAccess at the call. An implicit destructor that cannot reach the destructor of a direct base or of a member (an array of them, an implicit one that is blocked in turn) makes every use of it S150 "'H7': its implicit destructor cannot access the private destructor of 'M7'" (12.4/5; Sema::implicitDestructorBlocked, checked as a member of the class and remembered by class; cl reports the use as cppparse does, clang the member). `throw r` of a class lvalue needs the copy constructor (15.1/5). A friend function template defined in a class template was not declared at all, so a call of it was S103 (valid C++98 refused) and the instantiations went unchecked: each instantiation of the class now defines the template for itself (14.5.3/5), a template of the namespace with that class's arguments and injected name in the scope of its signature and body (the using-directives of instantiateBoundFunctionTemplate), so `peek8(a, 1)` on a `CT8<int>` and on a `CT8<long>` are specialisations of two templates, named as cl names them. A name in parentheses, `(peek10)(f, 2)`, is not looked up by its arguments (3.4.2/1), so a friend that only its class declares is S103 there. A friend declared with a qualified name that names no function of that signature in a namespace (`friend int N::nothing(int);`, `friend int N::f(long);`, `friend int ::nothing8(int);`) is S116 (cl C2039), where it declared a function and left the class unchecked; `template <class T> friend void B::mt(T);` makes the specialisations of that member template friends, and the class is checked. sample-front/access-refused4.cpp (test 17: 27 diagnostics on the lines cl refuses, 25 of them clang's lines too; clang names the member for the three implicit destructors) and friend-refused.cpp (8 errors), and front2.cpp `accessrest::` (the valid forms of each, against cl at -O0 and -O2). Measured and left, because only one of the two compilers refuses: the signatures of function templates (`template <class T> A5::PT ft(T)` naming a private typedef: clang refuses it, cl accepts every form), a thrown object with a private destructor (clang only), and every specialisation of a befriended function template name counts as a friend (cl's rule; clang takes the one named). Also left: a class with a friend declared by a qualified name that only a function template or a class template's instantiation could match stays unchecked |
| G15 | (row G6's left item) an array parameter inside a function type has no symbol to remember that it was declared as an array: `int fp(int (*cb)(int[3]), int a[3])` is `?fp@@YAHP6AHPEAH@ZQEAH@Z` where cl writes `?fp@@YAHP6AHQEAH@Z0@Z`, so such a function does not link across the two compilers | fixed (2026-11-13 for the parameters of a declared function, 2026-11-18 for the rest), measured on the names in cl's objects (dumpbin). Sema::applyDeclarator records, for each parameter of a function it declares, which parameters of the function types inside that parameter's type were written as arrays (`Symbol::nestedArrays`, in the order the mangler meets them: a function type's result first, then each parameter before what is inside its own type), and SemaMangler::paramList spells those as const pointers as it did the function's own: `int fp(int (*cb)(int[3]), int a[3])` is `?fp@@YAHP6AHQEAH@Z0@Z`. cl takes `int (*)(int[3])` and `int (*)(int*)` for one type, written as its first use in the name was, so the second use is a back-reference whichever way it is written (`two(int (*a)(int[3]), int (*b)(int*))` is `P6AHQEAH@Z1`, `two2` with the parameters the other way round `P6AHPEAH@Z1`): the back-reference key spells the function types inside a type plainly (SemaMangler::paramType), while a function's own array parameter stays a type of its own (`mix(int (*cb)(int*), int a[3])` is `P6AHPEAH@ZQEAH`). Found with it and fixed: a parameter of reference to function type was spelled `AEA$$A6A...`, cl's is `A6A...` as the pointer's `P6A...` (`ref(int (&)(char* argv[]), char** v)` is `?ref@fnarr@@YAHA6AHQEAPEAD@ZPEAPEAD@Z`). front2.cpp `fnarr::` (test 17: fourteen names in the -O0 listing equal to those of cl's object, among them two levels of function types, `rep` with two back-references, a function type's result that is a pointer to function, a member function, a static one, a template instantiation; the program's output against cl at -O0 and -O2). Found with it: rows G18 and G19. 2026-11-18, the rest, each `Q` with cl: the function types in the type a declared function returns (`int (*pick(char c[2]))(int[3])` was `YAP6AHPEAH@ZQEAD@Z`, cl's is `YAP6AHQEAH@ZQEAD@Z`), a function type named through a typedef (`typedef int CB(int[3]); int t1(CB* cb)` is `P6AHQEAH@Z`, with `typedef int CBP(int*)` it is `P6AHPEAH@Z`, and `t4(CB*, CBP*)` writes the first and refers back to it) and a variable of such a type, a static data member too (`int (*gp)(int[3])` is `?gp@@3P6AHQEAH@ZEA`). The list of a declared function's parameters (`Symbol::nestedArrays`) is now kept for every declared entity: Sema::applyDeclarator tells the function part of a declared function, the last one applied, from the function parts of the type it returns and from those of a variable or a typedef, and lists the parameters of the latter in the caller's list, in the order the mangler meets them; a declaration made with a typedef starts its list with the typedef's (Sema::specArrays), a parameter typed with one as well. SemaMangler::function reads the list over the result type, SemaMangler::variable over the variable's type. front2.cpp `fnarr2::` (test 17: nine function names and eight variable names in the -O0 listing, those of cl's object; with them all 440 decorated names of cl's object of front2.cpp are in cppparse's; the output against cl at -O0 and -O2). Left, by design: cl has two type descriptors for the one type (`??_R0P6AHQEAH@Z@8` and `??_R0P6AHPEAH@Z@8`: its `typeid` of the two spellings compare unequal and a handler written one way does not catch a pointer thrown the other way), cppparse one, spelled plainly; and cl instantiates a function template once for each spelling of a deduced argument (`tf(gp)` and `tf(gp2)` are `??$tf@P6AHQEAH@Z@...` and `??$tf@P6AHPEAH@Z@...`) while its class templates take the two for one argument, spelled plainly, as cppparse does for both |

So a program's operator new sees them, and a failed allocation in a string or a container is a `bad_alloc` where `malloc`'s null pointer was written through. sample-lib/replacenew.cpp (test 15, against cl's library at -O0 and -O2): a string, a vector, a list, a deque, a map, a set and a valarray each allocate from the program's `operator new` and free through its `operator delete`, and with an `operator new` that fails a string, a list and a map throw `bad_alloc`. | G22 | (gap analysis item 6, row C3's left items) `<locale>` has no `codecvt`, no time facets (`time_get`, `time_put`), no monetary facets (`moneypunct`, `money_put`, `money_get`) and no `messages`, nor their `_byname` forms: a program that names one is S100 or S101 | fixed (2026-11-18) for the classic locale: `include/__facets`, which `<locale>` includes, has them all, and every locale has them without holding them (`__default_facet`, as for `num_put` and `num_get`), until the program installs its own. `codecvt<internT, externT, stateT>` converts nothing between two character types of one size (`codecvt<char, char, mbstate_t>`: noconv, 22.2.1.5.2) and otherwise one character to one of the same value, a wide character above 255 being an error (`codecvt<wchar_t, char, mbstate_t>` in the "C" locale). `messages` has no catalogues: `open` is negative, `get` the default. `time_put` writes each conversion with strftime (`%c` as `%x %X`, as cl's library, where the C runtime's is ctime's); `time_get` reads `hh:mm:ss`, `mm/dd/yy` (`date_order` is `mdy`), a weekday's or a month's name in full or its first three letters in either case, and a year of two digits (below 69: this century) or four. `moneypunct<charT, Intl>` is the "C" locale's (no point, separator, grouping, symbol or fraction; "" and "-"; symbol, sign, none, value); `money_put` and `money_get` follow 22.2.6.2.2 and 22.2.6.1.2 with the locale's moneypunct: grouping, the fraction filled with zeros, the first character of a sign in its place and the rest at the end, `space` written as the fill character and the padding of an internal adjustment at `space` or `none`; reading takes the negative format, the symbol where `showbase` asks for it (otherwise only when more of the format follows), checks the groups, and stores the digits as read. sample-lib/facets.cpp (test 15, against cl's library at -O0 and -O2: 58 lines over the four families, a moneypunct of the program's with a symbol, signs of one and two characters, grouping and two fraction digits, wide forms). Not compared, where cl's library and 22.2 differ or 22.2 leaves it open: the values a failed `time_get` leaves, an amount without a sign where both signs are non-empty (cl reads it as positive, 22.2.6.1.2/3 requires the sign), a currency symbol at the end of an amount read without `showbase` (cl consumes it, the standard only if more follows). Left: named locales other than "C" and "" (the `_byname` facets take those two names), and `locale::global` does not call `setlocale` |
| G23 | (found by row G17's probes) a second handler for the same type in one try block compiled without a word, and so did a handler after `catch (...)`. Measured first, one probe per case (68 of them), with cl /permissive- /W4 and clang -std=c++98 -pedantic -Wall. The same type, references and top-level cv-qualifiers apart: cl refuses it (C2312 `catch (int)` twice, a typedef of it, `const D&` then `D&`, `const int*` then `int*`; C2315 `int` then `const int&`; C2313 `const int&` then `int`), clang warns for the identical types and says nothing once a reference or a qualifier differs. A class after its base, by value, by reference or by pointer: cl warns (C4286), also where the base is private, protected or ambiguous and for `B*` then `const D*`, where the earlier handler does not catch what the later one does (15.3/3); clang warns for the public unambiguous ones, and for `B*` then `const D*` too. Both are silent on `D&` then `const D&`, `void*` then `int*` and `B*&` then `D*`; cl alone on `B` then `D&`. `catch (...)` not last: cl C2311, clang "catch-all handler must come last", for a second `catch (...)` as well | fixed (2026-11-19). C++98 15.3/5 allows a handler that an earlier one hides ("never executed"), and no rule asks for a diagnostic, so the unit compiles as before and Sema::handlerOrder warns, once per hidden handler, on the handler's line with cl's wording and number: S162 "'const int&': reference is caught by 'int' on line 16: the handler is never entered (cl C2315)". Given where cl reports the pair and the earlier handler really catches all the later one would: the same type (C2312, C2313, C2315; a typedef and the instantiation of a template too), a class after an unambiguous public base of it and a pointer to one after a pointer to the other with no qualifier lost (C4286), a pointer after one to the same type with more qualifiers (C2312). Not given for cl's C4286 on a private, protected or ambiguous base and on `B*` before `const D*` (the later handler can be entered), nor where cl is silent (`D&` before `const D&`, `B` before `D&`, `void*` before another pointer: dead handlers, no warning). A handler after `catch (...)` is ill-formed (15.3/6) and was accepted: now S161 "a handler after 'catch (...)' on line 4: '...' must be the last handler of its try block", on the line cl reports, in a function-try-block too. sample-front/handler-order.cpp (test 17: compiles, eleven S162 on the lines cl reports and no other; the lines for the private and ambiguous base, the lost qualifier and the three silent pairs carry none) and handler-refused.cpp (three S161). The own sources, libcpp and the samples compile without S162. Left: a handler for a pointer or reference to an incomplete class (15.3/1; cl C2310, clang an error) is accepted, and `catch (void ())` after `catch (void (*)())` goes unreported |
| G24 | (row M7's left item) the table model's cost of a throw out of a function with many regions: M7 stage 5 measured a throw out of one frame with 1,000 objects alive at 3.1 ms in the table model against 0.10 ms in `--eh=cl` and 0.06 ms in cl's own code, and a throw through ten frames at 7.1 us against 5.9 and 5.4; cause not looked into | fixed (2026-11-19), in the runtime alone (runtime/cppparse_rt.cpp; no object byte differs, every program's output is the same). The probe written again (three units: 1,000 objects in one frame, a recursion ten deep with an object each, constructor, destructor and throw out of line; cl /EHsc, `--eh=table` and `--eh=cl` at -O0 and -O2) gave 2,966 us and 7.0 us. Measured with cycle counters in the runtime: the landing pad of each object sent the exception on through `cppparse_unwind_destroy`, `cppparse_rethrow` and a whole dispatch, 1,000 dispatches for the frame, and of a dispatch's 7,000 cycles 5,100 were `rangePad` reading the range table from its front (2,500 entries a time: the pads lie behind the body, so a pad's call site is behind nearly all of them: quadratic in the objects of a frame), 800 the lookups and virtual unwinds of four frames, three of them the runtime's own, and 900 RtlRestoreContext, the pad and the destructor. Now `rangePad` is a binary search; `cppparse_unwind_destroy` finds the landing pad of its own call site and, when the frame has one (the object built before), returns into it by its return address, with the frame and the callee-saved registers the pad expects, the function's range table noted by the dispatch that entered the frame (no lookup); and the walk starts in the runtime function the generated code called (one runtime frame to pass, not three). Best of three runs on the idle machine, -O0 / -O2: out of the 1,000-object frame 74.4 / 76.2 us (before 2,966 / 2,967; `--eh=cl` 98.5 / 97.5; cl 61.4 / 60.7), through ten frames 5.53 / 5.64 us (before 7.00 / 7.16; `--eh=cl` 5.77 / 5.80; cl 6.12 / 5.72); the paths without a throw as before (4.46 / 4.57 us and 78.5 / 57.1 ns). The 1,000-object unit's sections, unchanged: table 47,924 bytes of code and 16,100 of unwind tables at -O0 and 62,010 and 16,116 at -O2, `--eh=cl` 40,980 and 24,184, 55,082 and 24,200, cl 59,992 and 30,864 (/Od), 42,672 and 9,955 (/O2). eh.ps1 `ehManyObjects` (18 objects in a frame, an inner block, a destructor that throws and catches on its own between two pads, a value the handler reads; cl's output in every model at -O0..-O3; the probe prints 94 lines now) and harness.ps1 in both models. docs/design-table-exceptions.md, section 6. Left: a pad with more than one cleanup still costs a dispatch (few to a frame); noticed on the way, not looked into: the 1,000-object function's code is larger at -O2 than at -O0 in both of cppparse's models (62,010 against 47,924 bytes), and the range table holds 4,095 pads and 1 MB of code per function at most (the object writer refuses more). 2026-11-20: the second change, the return into the next pad by a written return address, is replaced by row G30 |

| G26 | (row M7's follow-up) `--eh=cl` writes `__CxxFrameHandler3`'s tables (FH3): FuncInfo, UnwindMap, TryBlockMap, HandlerType and IpToStateMap entries of fixed size, an image-relative relocation for every reference. They are the larger part of what the model costs. sample-eh's probe.cpp at -O2 on this tree: `.pdata` + `.xdata` 17,104 bytes against the table model's 6,828; of the 10,276 more the maps are 7,060 (10,240 against the CPPX tables' 3,180) and the funclets' entries and unwind records 3,216 (row G27). cl 19.52 writes `__CxxFrameHandler4`'s tables (FH4) by default and FH3's with `/d2FH4-`. Measured 2026-11-19, /O2 /EHsc: p.cpp's maps and FuncInfos 404 bytes against 968 (`.xdata` 636 against 896 and 320 of FuncInfos in `.rdata`), probe.cpp's 5,543 against 9,504 (58 %), with `.pdata` and the unwind records 10,043 against 14,492 (69 %). What FH4 is, from cl's /FAs listing and the toolset's `ehdata4_export.h`: the FuncInfo is a header byte (1 a catch funclet, 8 an UnwindMap, 10h a TryBlockMap, 20h /EHs) and a 4-byte image-relative address, still with its relocation, for each map the byte names; the numbers in the maps (counts, states, frame offsets, adjectives) are compressed, 1 to 5 bytes with the length in the first byte's low bits; the IP map holds an address as its difference from the entry before, counted from the function's start, and the state plus one (the states themselves are not differences); an UnwindMap entry is the distance in bytes back to the entry it unwinds to, with a kind in the low two bits, and for a destructor of an object in the frame or through a pointer in the frame it names the destructor itself and the frame offset, no funclet (14 of p.cpp's 17 cleanup funclets are still in cl's object with nothing that refers to them; a member at an offset behind `this` keeps its funclet); type descriptors, destructors, funclets and the handlers' maps stay 4-byte addresses; a catch funclet with states of its own has its own FuncInfo (header bit 1, its UnwindMap and IP map, the frame offset), one without has no handler in its unwind record (8 bytes where FH3 has 16); most functions lose the `-2` store (docs/design-cl-exceptions.md, section 3) | open; not started. Plan: `eh/EhClFuncInfo` gets a second writer for FH4 beside the FH3 one, the personality becomes `__CxxFrameHandler4` (VCRUNTIME140_1.dll in the DLL runtime), chosen by an option so that both can be compared; the runtime's `clFrameHasState` (cppparse_rt.cpp) reads both formats already; cl's own bytes for the probes are the reference where the layouts allow it. Expected by cl's ratio: probe.cpp's 10,240 bytes of maps become about 6,000, which with row G27 leaves its unwind data near 11,400 bytes against the table model's 6,828: not within the 3 % of row M7 for that file; the own sources (+2.5 % at -O2, +7.5 % at -O0) and the samples (+5 to +6 %) are to be measured (docs/design-cl-exceptions.md, stage 5's table). A subsystem's change: design section first, then review |
| G27 | (row M7's follow-up) the unwind entries and records of `--eh=cl`'s funclets. Every handler funclet and every cleanup funclet with a frame is a function of its own to Windows: a `.pdata` entry of 12 bytes and an unwind record (a handler's names the personality and the FuncInfo again, 20 bytes). probe.cpp at -O2: 248 entries where the table model has 127 (63 handler funclets, 55 cleanup funclets with a frame; 47 more cleanups are `lea rcx, [rdx+n]` / `jmp destructor` without an entry), 3,888 bytes of unwind records against 2,124. cl 19.52 does the same in its objects, FH3 and FH4 alike, measured 2026-11-19: an entry and a record of its own for every catch funclet and every cleanup funclet with a frame, none for `lea`/`jmp` cleanups, no chained record in p.cpp (11 of probe.cpp's 189 records are chained; which, was not looked at). Nothing is shared in the object: the first text of this row had it from memory that cl's funclets use their function's record, and that is not so. Two differences were measured. (1) cl writes each record as a COMDAT `.xdata` section and link /OPT:ICF (the default of a release link) folds equal ones: p.cpp's 15 records become 11 with FH3 (236 bytes to 180), 13 with FH4 (220 to 204); cppparse writes the records of a code section into one `.xdata` section and its 19 stay 19 (344 bytes; the images have a main each). Counted by content in the objects, probe.cpp's 248 records are 119 different ones (3,888 bytes to 2,404; at -O0 321 are 122, 4,512 to 2,420), and the table model's 127 are 53 (2,124 to 1,128; at -O0 175 are 36, 2,604 to 752): the handlers of one function have the same prologue, personality and FuncInfo, and most cleanups the same frame. (2) cl has fewer cleanups with a frame: a member's destructor in a constructor is `mov rcx, [rdx+n]` / `add rcx, k` / `jmp destructor` in cl, a funclet with a frame, an entry and a record in cppparse (p.cpp: 6 cleanups with a frame where cl has 2; cl's two call operator delete, two of cppparse's are the members of Two) | open; not started. Plan: (1) the object writer keeps one copy of equal unwind records and lets the `.pdata` entries share it, in every model (link does the same to cl's; `eh/EhUnwindInfo`): 1,484 bytes of probe.cpp's 17,104 in cl's model, 996 of 6,828 in the table model; (2) a cleanup that is one destructor call on an address the frame holds becomes a funclet without a frame, `mov rcx, [rdx+n]` / `jmp`: 12 bytes of `.pdata`, 8 to 12 of record and the prologue less for each; count first how many of the 55 are of that kind. The `.pdata` entries of the handlers and of the cleanups that call more than a destructor stay: cl has them too. Gates: eh.ps1 and ehx.ps1 in the model, harness.ps1 in both models, the debugger's stack through a funclet. With row G26: FH4 names such destructors in the map and needs no funclet for them at all |
| G28 | (row C3's left item, row G22's "Left"; the plan was in an uncommitted note of 2026-11-18) named locales: `std::locale("de-DE")` and every name but "C" and "" throws `runtime_error`, the `_byname` facets take those two names only, and `locale::global` does not call `setlocale`. 22.1.1.2/2 leaves the set of names to the implementation, so this is a difference from cl's library, not a breach | fixed (2026-11-20), for every name the C runtime's `_create_locale` takes. A named locale is the classic locale with fifteen facets made of one `__named_locale` (`include/__facets`): the C runtime's locale object of the name and a copy of its lconv. It is made on the calling thread's own C locale (`_configthreadlocale`, `setlocale`, `localeconv`, `_get_current_locale`), which leaves the program's C locale and the other threads alone and costs a sixth of `_create_locale` (measured: 2 to 6 us against 30 to 36 for the object alone). The facets: `ctype_byname<char>` (a table of the code page's 256 characters from `_isctype_l` with ctype_base's bits, which the streams scan as they do the classic one, and tables of `_toupper_l` and `_tolower_l`), `ctype_byname<wchar_t>` (the case by `_towupper_l` and `_towlower_l`, with U+0080 to U+00FF converted as Latin-1 has them, where the C runtime looks the wide character up in the code page's table and U+00FF became U+009F; widen and narrow through the code page, `_mbstowcs_l` and `_wctomb_s_l`), `numpunct_byname` (the lconv, its wide members for wchar_t), `collate_byname` (`_strcoll_l` and `_strxfrm_l`, the wide forms for wchar_t; a string the code page cannot hold is ordered behind the others, so the order stays one order), `moneypunct_byname` in its four forms (the lconv as cl's library reads it: the 30 formats of sep_by_space, cs_precedes and sign_posn were measured on cl's `moneypunct_byname` with a patched lconv), `time_put_byname` (`_strftime_l`, `_wcsftime_l`), `time_get_byname` (the names of `%A`, `%a`, `%B` and `%b` and the order and separator of `%x`'s numbers, asked of the C runtime at the facet's first use) and `codecvt_byname<wchar_t, char, mbstate_t>` (`_mbtowc_l`, `_wctomb_s_l`). The `_byname` facets take the same names. `locale(other, name, cats)` and `locale(other, one, cats)` carry every facet of 22.1.1.1.1's table for each category from its source, the program's own facets among them. Names: a locale is named as it was asked for ("German" stays "German"; "" is the user's locale, the C runtime's "", and is named ""), and a locale made of two has the name of the one the categories come from, which is cl's choice where 22.1.1.2 says only when there is a name (`locale(classic, de, numeric).name()` is "de-DE", and the locale compares equal to `de`). `locale::global` calls `setlocale(LC_ALL, name)` for a locale with a name (22.1.1.5). Since the program's C locale changes now, the classic locale no longer asks the C library where that reads it: its `ctype` table and case conversions are ASCII's, written out (they were `isupper`, `toupper` and the others, of whatever the C locale was at the time), and once `locale::global` has given the C library another locale its `time_put` writes with the C runtime's "C" locale object and `num_get` hands `strtod` that locale's decimal point (until then both ask the C library as they did, at no cost). `<ctime>`'s `time`, `localtime`, `gmtime`, `difftime` and `mktime` are inline functions (they were macros, and `time` renamed `std::locale::time` wherever `<ctime>` came second). Test 15, sample-lib/namedlocale.cpp against cl's library at -O0 and -O2, 244 lines: for "de-DE", "German_Germany.1252", "en-US" and "French_France.1252" (the C runtime of this machine accepts all four; the program asks `_create_locale` first and leaves a missing one out, in both builds alike) the name, numpunct for char and wchar_t and the streams that write and read with it, `ctype<char>` over all 256 characters (how many are in each of the eleven classes, where both conversions lead), `ctype<wchar_t>` on ten characters with widen and narrow, codecvt in and out, collate and its keys on fifteen words (French orders "côte" before "coté", German after) and a sort, time_put with its conversions, time_get (names, a date, a time, a year), the four moneypuncts, money_put and money_get, each for wchar_t too; eleven `_byname` facets; ten combinations of categories, their names and equality; `locale::global` with printf ("1,50"), a new stream written and read, the classic locale unchanged under it, and "C" again; eight threads making 800 locales. Every line is cl's. By probes beyond the test: `ctype<char>` equals cl's in every class and conversion, and the wide case conversions equal cl's over every wide character, in eleven locales; of 24 other locales twelve print what cl's build prints (fr-FR, en-GB, de_DE, German, nl-NL, pt-BR, de-CH, da-DK, it-IT, es-ES, hu-HU, en-IN), and the others differ where cl's library writes a locale's names through the program's code page ("?" for Russian letters, "brezen" for "březen", a Latin-1 byte in a UTF-8 locale) and cannot read them back, gives sv-SE the order dmy for its "1998-03-03", or meets a calendar that is not the Gregorian one (ar-SA, fa-IR: `date_order` is no_order here and a date is not read). Not cl's, on purpose, and checked for cppparse's build alone (`namedlocale own`, seven lines): `get_date` reads `%x` with the locale's separator (cl's library stops at the first dot of a German date); a range with a null character is compared beyond it (cl's stops there); the classes of wide characters are the C runtime's (cl's library calls a tab and U+00A0 printable); a bad name throws from a `_byname` facet and whatever the categories (22.1.1.2; cl's: neither); a locale made of one without a name has none (22.1.1.2; cl's names it for the locale named); `global` of `locale(classic, "de-DE", numeric)` sets the C locale by the locale's name, the whole of it (cl's library only LC_NUMERIC, though the whole for the same locale made of two objects); no thread's C locale is disturbed while the eight threads make locales. Cost, measured with a benchmark of the same paths under the old and the new headers (-O2, best of five, the machine otherwise idle): the classic paths are as they were within the 3 % by which two builds of one path differ (three ints written 371 and 369 ns, two doubles 997 and 999, a long, two doubles and an int read 1,633 and 1,687, and 1,683 and 1,688 in a program without the one test that names a locale, eleven words read 706 and 728, there 732 and 722, `locale()`, a copy and `locale("C")` 62 and 61); the classic case conversions are faster (toupper and tolower of 54 characters 455 to 299 ns, wide 969 to 282), the classic `time_put` 4 % slower (four conversions 1,530 to 1,588 ns: a call more for each). Named: `locale("de-DE")` made and destroyed 9.6 us (cl's library 62), with each facet used once 27 (cl's 64; the names time_get asks for are most of it), a German stream writing an int and a double 0.91 us (1.57), a copy and a locale of two 0.59 us (37), toupper and tolower of 54 characters 93 ns (10.7 us), the collation of two words 264 ns (193: the ranges are copied to end them with a null character). A program that names no locale is 3 to 4 KB smaller (ten samples: the classic ctype calls no C function); one that names a locale links the fifteen facets and the C runtime's locale code, 128 KB (locale.cpp: 459 KB where cl's build is 554 KB), and its unit compiles in 1.9 s where it took 1.0. The headers stay strict C++98 (clang -std=c++98 -pedantic -Wall over the three locale samples: nothing but long long in `<__config>`; strict98.ps1 unchanged). Left: `messages` has no catalogues under any name; the wide file streams convert through the C runtime, not through the locale's codecvt; `get_time` reads hh:mm:ss in every locale and no AM or PM, and a date with a month's name is not read (both as cl's library); a program that calls `setlocale` itself is not seen by the library: its C locale shows in the dates the classic locale writes and stops a floating-point number the streams read at the point, as before; a unit that makes a locale of a name must include `<locale>`, as C++98 says (without it `std::locale::__named_imp` is unresolved at link, for "C" too); noticed and left: the classic locale's wide `is()` asks the C runtime nine times per character, and cl's classic `ctype<wchar_t>` converts the case of every letter where C's "C" locale and cppparse's convert ASCII's |
| G29 | (row G23's leftovers) the type of a handler's exception-declaration. (1) `catch (void ())` stopped the compiler with an access violation, a named `catch (int f(int))` declared a function `f` in the handler (a call of it ended in the object writer's internal error), and `catch (int a[3])` kept the array type: its variable was an array and its match the type `int[3]`, which no thrown object has, so the handler was never entered. 15.3/2 makes such a declaration the pointer, as for a parameter. (2) a handler for an incomplete type, or for a pointer or a reference to one, compiled (but for an incomplete class by value, S113): 15.3/1 forbids them, `void*` with any qualifiers apart | done (2026-11-19): `Sema::handlerType` (SemaExpr.cpp) gives the declaration's type: an array is the pointer to its element, a function the pointer to it, before the variable is made, so the variable, the handler's match in the three models and the order check of row G23 see the pointer (`catch (int*)` then `catch (int [3])` is S162 now, as cl's C2312). An incomplete type (a class that is declared only, also a class template's specialisation that cannot be instantiated; `void`; an array of unknown bound), a pointer to one and a reference to one are the error S163, `'Inc*': a handler cannot catch a pointer to the incomplete type 'Inc' (C++98 15.3/1; cl accepts it)`; in a template, when it is instantiated. Taken, as by cl and clang: `void*` with any qualifiers, a pointer to a pointer to an incomplete class, a reference to such a pointer, a pointer to a member of one. Measured, 30 probes (cl 19.52 /permissive- /W4, clang -std=c++98 -pedantic): cl refuses the incomplete type itself and `void` with C2687, a reference to an incomplete class with C2316 (whose text is about the destructor), and takes a pointer to an incomplete class and a pointer to an array of unknown bound without a word (the C2310 this row's first text named is not what this cl says), a reference to an array of unknown bound or to a function with the warning C4843; clang refuses every one of them. A function or array handler after the pointer is cl's C2312 and a warning of clang. Tests: handler-refused.cpp (11 more errors, the taken forms in one function), handler-order.cpp (two more warnings, and the named forms without one), eh.ps1 `ehAdjustedHandlers` (a thrown array, `&function`, a function by name, a `const int*`, a double, against `catch (int a[3])`, `catch (int f(int))`, `catch (const int a[])`: cl's lines in every model at -O0..-O3). | G30 | (row G24's second change; the user, 2026-11-20: "G24's fix overwrites a return address on the stack: that should be resolved and controlled") `cppparse_unwind_destroy` looked up the landing pad of its own call site and returned into it by writing its return address. A return to another address than its call's is what a shadow stack (CET) refuses, and nothing bounded the write | done (2026-11-20): the generated code goes to the pad. The next pad of a frame is the pad of the innermost region open at the pad's own call, which the code generator knows: the pad of an object in a region of its function ends with `call cppparse_unwind_destroy_next`, a new entry of the runtime that destroys the object while the exception is parked and returns, and `jmp` to that region's pad, the block the ranges name or the dispatch stub (`EhPads::isDestroy`, `destroyNext`, `next` in cppparse/eh/EhTable; `X86Function::nextPad`, `regionPadBlock`); a pad in no region keeps `cppparse_unwind_destroy`, which does not return. `X86Function::orderPads` lays such a pad right before the pad it goes on at (newest object first), so the jump falls away: the 1,000-object unit has 47,924 and 62,010 bytes of code at -O0 and -O2, as before. The runtime lost `rangeTableAt` and the function it noted; it reads its return address in one place (`cppparse_unwind_enter`) and writes none; objects compiled before call `cppparse_unwind_destroy` in every pad and get a dispatch each. IR, optimiser and allocator unchanged. Best of three runs (another lane building on the machine: cl's and `--eh=cl`'s figures are a few percent above row G24's idle ones), -O0 / -O2: out of the 1,000-object frame 13.4 / 12.4 us (row G24 74.4 / 76.2; `--eh=cl` 103.2 / 105.0; cl 63.2 / 65.1), through ten frames 5.54 / 5.65 us (`--eh=cl` 6.01 / 6.09; cl 6.22 / 6.11), the paths without a throw 4.73 / 4.72 us and 81.0 / 59.9 ns. Checks: the suite reads front.cpp's listings (each call of the new entry is followed by a jump or by the next pad's label) and the runtime's source (its return address read in one place, written in none); eh.ps1 `ehPadChain` (chains that end in `catch (...)` and in clauses of which none matches, an array and a conditional temporary among the pads, a loop; cl's lines in every model at -O0..-O3; the probe prints 106 lines); harness.ps1 in both models. Not claimed: no run under a shadow stack (this machine's processor has no CET); a frame's first pad is entered by `RtlRestoreContext` as before, limit 2 of docs/design-cl-exceptions.md. Left: a pad with more than one cleanup (enter, destructors, leave, `cppparse_rethrow`) and a dispatch stub of which no clause matches still go on by a dispatch; both could go on by the same jump |
| G31 | (found by row G30's first harness run, 2026-11-20) `<vector>`'s range members take pointer ranges only: `v.insert(v.end(), w.rbegin(), w.rend())` is S107 (no matching function among 3 candidates), where 23.2.4.1 and 23.2.4.3 declare the constructor, `assign` and `insert` as member templates over any input iterator. The header's comment says so (the iterators of vector and basic_string are pointers, so the common calls compile); a range of list, set, map or deque iterators, of reverse iterators or of istream iterators does not. cl's library takes them | fixed (2026-11-20, 6e6c975). Measured first, 253 probes, one container, one member and one kind of range each (pointers to const and to non-const, the iterators of a vector, a list, a set, a deque and a map, reverse iterators of a vector and of a list, a const list's, istream_iterator, istreambuf_iterator, two ints), built by cl with its own headers, by cl against include/ (the referee between the library and the compiler) and by cppparse: cl took all, cppparse refused 82 (vector's assign and insert for every range that is not pointers, S107; assign and insert of two ints for deque and list, S119; map's range constructor and insert, which did not exist; multiset and multimap, which did not exist; `vector<double> v(0u, 0)`, ambiguous through the non-template pointer overloads, S106). The constructor, assign and insert of vector, deque and list, the five iterator members of basic_string and the range constructor and insert of set, multiset, map and multimap are member templates over any input iterator; `__range_category` and `__range_kind` (`<__iterator>`) say what the two arguments are and the member passes a null pointer of that type to its worker: two integers are a count and a value (23.1.1/9), pointers to the element type are copied as before (memcpy for the bytewise types), a forward range is measured once and placed with one allocation, an input range is read element by element (vector and deque insert it from a container of its own, so an exception of the iterator changes nothing). list builds the nodes of a count or a range apart and splices them in (23.2.2.3), deque inserts at the front without moving an element and copies all its elements before it destroys one when it reallocates, basic_string takes a pointer range as a pointer and a length; `__tree`'s iterator has the member types of iterator_traits, set and map have rbegin and rend, multiset and multimap are new (equal keys in the order they came). The compiler was in the way three times, fixed in Sema: of two conversions to bases the nearer base is better (13.3.3.2/4); a partial specialisation matches only the argument list its pattern spells (14.5.4.1: `X<T, yes>` was taken for `X<int, no>`); a nested template argument is deduced as it is (14.8.2.4: `Box<U>` from `Box<const int>`). The optimizer inlines a function that only passes its arguments on to one call whatever its size, so at -O2 `v.insert(v.end(), w.begin(), w.end())` is the call of the worker with the old body (15 instructions at the call site became 17, the null tag; at -O0 one forwarding call more). All 253 probes compile and print what cl's builds print; harness.ps1 in both models on the merge into main. To do: the conversion-type-id through the class's own scope, and with a pointer to member declarator |
| G44 | (found by row M10's stage 4) a qualified name does not find a member of the named namespace's unnamed namespace: `namespace p { namespace { struct H { int a; }; } } p::H g;` is "unknown type name 'p'" (3.4.3.2: the unnamed namespace's using-directive counts for qualified lookup) | open: valid C++98 refused. To do: qualified lookup through the using-directives of the named namespace |
| G45 | (found by the review of row M10's stage 4, 2026-11-25) a class that names a virtual base before the polymorphic base whose function it overrides does not override in that base's table: `struct VR { virtual int r(); int vr; }; struct P1 { virtual int r(); int p1; }; struct J2 : virtual VR, P1 { int r(); int j; };` leaves slot 0 of `??_7J2@@6BP1@@@` to `?r@P1@@UEAAHXZ` where cl has `?r@J2@@UEAAHXZ` and an adjustor in VR's table; `Symbol::thisAdjust` is 32 where cl's this adjustor is 0. With the bases the other way round (`J : P1, virtual VR`) table and adjustor are right | fixed (2026-11-26). The cause: `Sema::completeClass` finds the function a member overrides by going through the bases in the order they are named (`over`, the first one found), and it gave the member the slot of the primary table (the table at offset 0: that of the first non-virtual base with a vfptr there) only when that slot held `over` itself, or an overrider of the name. That covers every class whose primary base is the first base searched. With a virtual base named before it, or a base whose only vfptr lies in its virtual base (`W : VL, P1` with `VL : virtual VR`), `over` is the virtual base's function and `P1::r` in the primary table is neither: the slot stayed `P1::r`, and with no slot at 0 the function was compiled to take this at the virtual base's vfptr (adjustor 32, the plain slot of VR's table). With the bases the other way round `over` is `P1::r`, which is why that order was right. Now, when the test gives no slot of the primary table, that table itself is searched for a function the member overrides (`overridesExactly`: the name, the signature, each parameter's own type, a conversion function's type; for a member declared in the class, no constructor or destructor). The member takes that slot and is reached there with this at the object, and the code that was there gives the tables inside the virtual bases their adjustors (cl's vtordisp thunks where the class has a constructor). A slot the old test found is the slot now, so no class that had its override in the primary table gets other tables. The fault had more faces than the row named, all gone with it: a class that overrides a pure virtual function of such a base was refused as abstract (S151, where cl compiles it); a pointer to member `&J2::r` and a member pointer of P1 called on a J2 reached `P1::r`; in a mixed link cl's direct call `j.r()`, which passes the object's address, ran cppparse's body with a wrong this (it read past the object); the constructors and destructors saw `P1::r` through `P1*` while a derived object was built; `-g2` listed the adjustment 32 (design-codeview.md). Measured against cl 19.52 before and after (lane-scratch\2026-11-26\g45, probes a1 to a5, c6, x1 and the review's): the output of every call and every vftable of the -O0 listing, slot for slot with the adjustor thunks by name, against cl's /FAs listing. Tests: `examples/sample-front/vbase-first.cpp` (test 17): 27 groups, 90 lines, the same as cl's build at -O0 and -O2 in the three exception models, and 222 vftables equal to cl's (the shapes: several virtual bases first, a virtual base without virtual functions, the function in one base only or in a base of a base, further derived classes that override again or do not, the bases named again or virtually, a base whose only vfptr is its virtual base's, two polymorphic bases, new virtual functions, overloads and const, pure virtual functions, a plain base beside, two levels, a diamond, class templates, functions of the name that are not overridden, virtual destructors in one base or both, every constructor and destructor calling through each base with and without a vtordisp, covariant returns in place and moving, pointers to member functions, `dynamic_cast` and `typeid`, a throw across the call and out of a constructor that makes it); sample-layout FV, FP, FJ, FN, FL, FW (test 13's mixed link at -O0 to -O3: cl and cppparse build, call through every base and delete each other's objects, cl derives FK from FJ with cppparse's constructors running inside cl's, cl's direct call and both compilers' pointers to members); sample-dbg `mem::RunJ` (test 20: the program's calls, and DIA's lines of the member functions are cl's: `run` adjust 0, `stay` 32). With the fault put back (the compiler of a1fca66 on these tests) vbase-first.cpp is refused (S151 twice), the layout driver fails 20 expectations at each level (cl's direct call returns garbage) and test 20's program exits with 1. Of the 918 objects and listings of the own sources, libcpp, the runtime and the examples (153 units at -O0, -O2 and -O2 `--eh=cl`) 906 are the bytes the compiler of a1fca66 writes; the 12 others are the two units that got the shape for the tests, layout.cpp and types.cpp (before they got it: 918 of 918). An independent review (its own probes against cl, some 270 vftables equal to cl's) found the family right and the first form of the fix wrong: it took the primary table's slot by name and `sameSignature` as the other tables' slots are taken, and so overrode there what those comparisons wrongly take for the same function (rows G49, G50), which the order of the bases had kept apart; the exact comparison above is for that, and vbase-first.cpp `x1::` holds it. One such case is left, row G42's: Sema keeps no volatile on a member function, so `J : virtual VR, P1 { int h() volatile; }` with `VR::h() volatile` and `P1::h()` now overrides `P1::h` as well, as it did before with the bases the other way round. Found on the way and not this row's: G46 to G51 |
